# High Court for the State of Telangana, CRLA/1387/2007, 2015-06-17

Ì‡Ð‰›ÂÂÂK„K„—"ÂÂÂ¦„kkkkä\ä\ä\ä“ÈðD„ä\ä\ä\ÈðÄÜÐ¬`Ä$¼àäääÿÿÿÿTHE HON’BLE SRI JUSTICE U. DURGA PRASAD RAO
CRIMINAL APPEAL No.1387 of 2007 JUDGMENT: This Criminal Appeal is preferred by State represented by A.C.B through Eluru
Range aggrieved by the dismissal of C.C.No.43 of 1992 by the Special Judge for S.P.E and A.C.B cases, whereunder the learned Judge
acquitted the AO holding that G.V.Gopala Rao—Accused Officer (for short “A.O”) has not amassed assets disproportionate to his known
source of income as alleged by the prosecution. 2) The facts which led to file the present appeal can be succinctly stated thus: a) The AO
entered into Government service as Junior Engineer in the Irrigation Department, Kurnool Division on 02.06.1956 and he was promoted
as Deputy Executive Engineer (Assistant Engineer) on 10.01.1964 and again promoted as Executive Engineer on 05.12.1977 and
continued as such. While so, on the reliable information that AO indulged in various corrupt practices and acquired assets
disproportionate to his known sources of income during the check period 02.06.1956 to 08.11.1987, the State represented by Inspector
of Police, ACB, Eluru Range registered a case in Cr.No.8/ACB-ELR/87 and investigated into the matter and laid charge sheet against
AO for the offence under Section 5(2) r/w 5(i)(e) of Prevention of Corruption Act, 1947 (for short “P.C.Act,1947”) and learned Special
Judge took cognizance of the same and registered as C.C.No.43 of 1992. b) The trial Court after considering the evidence on record
found the AO amassed disproportionate assets worth Rs.3,50,163/- and convicted him for the offence under Section 13(2) r/w 13(1) (e) of
Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988 (for short “P.C.Act, 1988”) and sentenced to suffer RI for a period of one year and six months and also
to pay a fine of Rs.3,50,000/- in default to suffer SI for ten months. c) Aggrieved, the AO preferred Crl.A.No.432 of 1998 before the High
Court and inter alia contended that the trial Court’s conducting trial under Sec.5 (2) r/w 5 (1) (e) of P.C. Act, 1947 i.e, Old Act but
convicting him for the offence under Sec.13 (2) r/w 13(1) (e) of P.C. Act, 1988 i.e, New Act is contrary to law which was upheld by the
High Court. The learned Judge while remanding the appeal observed as follows: “In the present case, framing of charges and
conducting of trial were under the provisions of Old Act and the learned Special Judge rendered the judgment by convicting the
appellant under Section 13(1) (e) r/w 13(2) of the New Act by gaining an impression that Section 13(1) (e) of the New Act is similar to
Section 5(1) (e) of the Old Act. Since the learned Special Judge invoked a wrong provision in convicting the appellant, it cannot be said
that the entire trial is liable to be vitiated. The conviction of the appellant can only be held illegal. When once the conviction of the
appellant is set aside on the ground of illegality in invoking the provisions of the New Act, the matter has to be necessarily remanded to
the Special Court for considering the case on merits whether the prosecution proved the guilt of the appellant beyond all reasonable
doubt under Section 5(1) (e) of the 1947 Act and to pass appropriate orders. Since this case is standing on a different footing than the
cases covered by the above decisions, I am inclined to set aside the conviction and sentence imposed on the appellant and remand the
matter to the trial Court for fresh disposal according to law.” After remanding the matter back to the trial Court, the learned Successor
Judge on rehearing found that the AO has not amassed the disproportionate assets and thus acquitted him of the charges levelled
against him. Hence the appeal by State. 3) Heard arguments of Sri M.B.Thimma Reddy, learned Special Public Prosecutor-cum-Standing Counsel for A.C.B (for short “Spl.P.P.”) and Sri T.Bali Reddy, learned Senior Counsel representing for Sri K.Suresh Reddy,
learned counsel for respondent/A.O. 4) As can be seen, the trial judge and his successor came to different conclusions before and after
remand of the matter as follows: S.NoParticularsTrial judge finding Rs. Ps.Successor judge finding Rs. Ps.1.a) Assets:11,24,327-
0010,09,661-00b) Income:9,29,288-33 1,85,857-67 (20% added as per G.O) 11,15,146.0011,68,488-00 1,16,848-00 (10% added as per
case law) 12,85,336-00c) Expenditure:3,40,981-002,58,315-00Result: a-(b-c)Disproportionate Assets of 3,50,162-00Savings exceeding
assets by 17,360-00 Thus the above are the divergent findings in the two judgments rendered by the trial Courts before and after
remand. It is trite doctrine of law that the earlier judgment is superseded by the judgment after remand. Hence, the point for determination
in this appeal is: “Whether the judgment of the trial Court after remand is factually and legally sustainable”? 5) POINT: As can be seen,
the main reason for this High Court to remand the matter to trial Court for fresh disposal in Crl.A.No.432 of 1998 is that the offence was
allegedly committed when PC Act, 1947 was in force and charges were framed and trial was also conducted under the said Act but
conviction was passed under Section 13(2) r/w 13(1)(e) of PC Act, 1988 and that the learned trial Judge invoked a wrong provision in
convicting the AO. Thus, while holding the trial as legal and valid, conviction was set aside and matter was remanded to the trial Court
for fresh disposal according to law. Hence, before proceeding with the appeal, it is pertinent to discuss the distinction between Section
5(1)(e) of PC Act, 1947 and Section 13(1)(e) of PC Act, 1988. Section 5(1)(e) of PC Act, 1947 reads thus: “Section 5 Criminal misconduct
in discharge of official duty:-- (1)A public servant is said to commit the offence of criminal misconduct. xx xx xx xx xx xx xx xx If he or any
person on his behalf is in possession or has, at any time during the period of his office, been in possession, for which the public servant
cannot satisfactorily account, of pecuniary resources or property disproportionate to his known sources of income.” Then, Section 13(1)
(e) of PC Act, 1988 and its Explanation reads thus: “Section 13 - Criminal, misconduct by a public servant: (1) A public servant is said to
commit the offence of criminal misconduct, — xx xx xx xx xx xx xx xx if he or any person on his behalf, is in possession or has, at any
time during the period of his office, been in possession for which the public servant cannot satisfactorily account, of pecuniary resources
or property disproportionate to his known sources of income. Explanation— For the purposes of this section, "known sources of income"
means income received from any lawful source and such receipt has been intimated in accordance with the provisions of any law, rules
or orders for the time being applicable to a public servant.” A comparative study of the above two provisions would show that both the
provisions are identical to each other except that an Explanation is added to Section 13(1)(e) explaining the meaning of the phrase
“known sources of income” employed in both the provisions. This difference is pointed out by Honourable Apex Court in its judgment in
Jagan M.Seshadri vs. State of Tamil Nadu. The Apex Court observed thus: “Para-7: xx xx… Section 13(1)(e) is materially different from
Section 5 of the 1947 Act. The presumption permitted to be raised under the explanation to Section 13(1)(e) was not available to be
raised under Section 5(1)(e) of the 1947 Act. This difference can have a material bearing on the case.” Since the instant case is one
under Section 5(1)(e) of PC Act 1947, this appeal has to be disposed of keeping the above distinction between the two provisions in
mind. 6) It may be noted that the below is the overview of the finding of the learned successor judge after remand in respect of the three
components i.e, assets, income and expenditure: TABLE-I ASSETS: Item No.As per charge sheetDefence pleaAs per

PW.20Total5,23,039-42Total2,13,635-50Total2,58,315.37 In this appeal the correctness of the above values as arrived by the trial Court
needs a thorough scrutiny. (I) ASSETS: As per impugned judgment, out of 18 Items of assets, the AO did not dispute Item Nos.4, 6, 7, 8,
9, 12, 13 and 14 before trial Court. During appeal, both parties did not dispute Items 2, 3, 10, 11 and 18. Hence, the remaining items are
taken up for hearing. 7) Item No.1 - Construction of double storied house at Plot No.12 in NTR Colony, Gunadala – Rs.4,00,500/-: The
prosecution valued this asset at Rs.4,00,500/-. Justifying the said valuation, PW.20—the I.O deposed that AO purchased a site with
super structure in the name of his wife—Manga Thayaru, who is his dependant under Ex.P.82—sale deed dated 12.02.1986 from Sri
J.Narayana Swamy for Rs.80,500/-. The cost of the site was Rs.30,500/- and the value of the super structure thereon was Rs.50,000/- as
per the recitals in the sale deed. Subsequently, AO constructed a double storied building thereon during 1986-87 which was valued at
Rs.3,20,000/- by PW.15—Deputy Executive Engineer, ACB. Thus the total value of the asset according to him Rs.4,00,500/-
(Rs.3,20,000/- + Rs.80,500/-). In Ex.P.72—explanation, AO admitted that he purchased house site along with house measuring 300
sq.yds at NTR Colony under Ex.P.82—sale deed for Rs.80,000/- from J.Narayana Swamy and spent about Rs.8,050/- towards stamp
and registration charges. He further admitted that he invested Rs.1.8 lakhs for further construction of ground floor and completion of first
floor by obtaining permission from the head of the department i.e, Engineer-in-chief. Thus, AO admitted the said asset to be his rather
than his wife. Ofcourse he disputed its value as got estimated by the I.O through PW.15. The contention of AO is that his wife got valued
the building under Ex.P.58—valuation report through one Sri B.Subbarow Gupta, Executive Engineer (Retd)., as per which its value is
only Rs.2.77 lakhs and the Income Tax Department also accepted the said valuation though it itself was on high side and therefore, the
I.O too ought to have accepted the said report. The further contention of AO was that the building was purchased in the year 1986 and
the vendor as per Exs.P.1 to P.3 applied for permission for construction of the building as early as in 1985 and the ground floor was
completed immediately thereafter and after purchase of the unfinished ground floor, the AO and his wife completed construction of the
remaining ground floor and first floor in the year 1986-1987. However, PW.15 adopted SSR’s of 1988 for furnishing report under
Exs.P.56 and P.57 and therefore the reports of PW.15 and Gangadhar Rao are erroneous due to excessive valuation. It must be noted
that the learned trial judge and his successor after remand have identically arrived the value of Item No.1 at Rs.3,50,500/-. For arriving
this value, they have accepted the valuation of PW.15 and his predecessor mentioned in Exs.P.56 and P.57 reports at Rs.3,20,000/-
towards the cost of the ground and first floor. To this amount, they added Rs.30,500/- towards cost of the site as per Ex.P.82—sale deed.
Thus, they arrived the total value of Item No.1 at Rs.3,50,500/-(Rs.3,20,000/- + Rs.30,500/-). In this regard, the trial Court in its earlier
judgment has not accepted Ex.P.58—valuation report submitted by AO on the ground that the valuer—Sri B.Subbarow Gupta, Executive
Engineer (Retd.) was not examined. Further, the trial Court basing on the evidence of PW.15, Exs.P.56 and P.57--reports held that SSR
rates pertaining to the year 1986-87 but not 1988 was adopted. Now the contention of learned Spl.P.P is that the value of the site and
unfinished ground floor as per Ex.P.82—was Rs.80,500/- and AO spent about Rs.3,20,000/- for completion of ground floor and also
construction of the first floor as per Ex.P.56 and P.57 reports and therefore the Court below ought to have added entire Rs.80,500/- to
Rs.3,20,000/-, to arrive at the total value of the building but instead the trial Court erroneously added only Rs.30,500/- towards cost of the
site leaving remaining Rs.50,000/- which was the cost of unfinished ground floor on the wrong assumption that its value was already
included in Ex.P.56 and P57 reports. On perusal of the record, the contention of learned Spl.P.P cannot be accepted. Precisely his
contention is that in Ex.P.56 and P.57—reports, the value of the site and unfinished ground floor was not taken into consideration and the
amount of Rs.3,20,000/- relates to the cost of finishing of the ground floor and construction of the second floor and therefore, to arrive at
the total value, the amount of Rs.80,500/- should be added. However, a perusal of Ex.P.56 and P.57 would show that PW.15 and his
predecessor had taken the cost of entire construction of the ground floor and also the first floor for arriving at the value at Rs.3,20,000/-. It
is evident from the mentioning such as cost of excavation for under-leaned pole foundation, RCC plinth beam, etc. Then the recitals in
Ex.P.82—sale deed would show that the total value of the site + the unfinished ground floor was Rs.80,500/-, of which the value of the
construction was Rs.50,000/-. If this entire Rs.80,500/- is added to Rs.3,20,000/-, as sought by Spl.P.P, it would amount to duplication of
the cost of ground floor. Therefore, both the judges below rightly added only Rs.30,500/- i.e, cost of site to Rs.3,20,000/- and arrived at
the total value of Item No.1 at Rs.3,50,500/-. I find no fault in it. 8) Item No.5 - MIG Plot No.75, V.Colony of 480 Sq. yards purchased by
AO from M/s.Vijayawada Co-operative Building Society, Vijayawada in the name of wife of AO– Rs.58.535/-: The prosecution claimed
that this site was purchased by AO from M/s. Vijayawada Cooperative Building Society under Ex.P.67—copies of bank receipts. The AO
denied and examined DW.3—Accountant of M/s. Vijayawada Cooperative Building Society who deposed that plot No.63 (Item No.4 of
the Assets) was allotted to G.Manga Thayaru, W/o. AO and whereas Plot No.75 was allotted to one Smt. L.Vijaya Laxmi W/o. L.Krishna
Rao C/o.Maganti, Nuzividu and to this effect the President of the Society issued a letter under Ex.D.19 to AO. Basing on this evidence,
the trial Court before and after remand has opined that Item No.5 of the Assets does not belong to AO. Now the contention of learned
Spl.P.P is that since Item No.4 of the Assets i.e, Plot No.63 was held to belong to AO, on the same analogy Item No.5 should also be
held as his Asset. This argument is quite preposterous. Item No.4 of the Assets i.e, plot No.63 was admittedly purchased by AO in the
name of his wife and was so deposed by DW.3. However, Item No.5 i.e, Plot No.75 stands in the name of one Smt. L.Vijaya Laxmi and
prosecution could not establish any connection even remotely between AO and said Vijaya Laxmi. Therefore, the trial Court rightly
omitted this Item from the Assets of AO. 9) ITEM No.15 – Purchase of Units in Unit Trust of India – Rs.48,530.61: As per I.O, AO
purchased Units worth Rs.11,939.57 from Unit Trust of India under Ex.D.3—certificate in the name of his wife and Units worth Rs.36,591
in his name under Ex.D.4—certificate. The contention of AO is that he invested only Rs.15,000/- and Exs.D.3 and D.4 and other
certificates were allotted by way of accumulated dividends on the original certificates and therefore, I.O showed an excess amount of
Rs.33,530.61 (Rs.48,530.61 minus Rs.15,000/-). Basing on the evidence of PW.13—Staff Officer of UTI and also the admission of
PW.20—I.O that he did not obtain any letter from UTI to show that the AO initially purchased units worth Rs.48,530.61, the trial Court in
its judgments before and after remand upheld the contention of I.O and valued the Item No.15 of the Assets at Rs.15,000/-. In this appeal,
the contention of Spl.P.P is that even if AO’s version is correct, his initial investment of Rs.15,000/- in UTI has fetched added value over
a period of time and by the relevant time the Unit Value increased to Rs.48,530.61 under Exs.D.3 and D.4 and therefore, the total amount
has to be taken as asset. I am unable to accept this contention. As per the evidence of PW.13, Ex.P.46 is a consolidated certificate

containing Units purchased by the AO at various dates. So his evidence implies that the Units purchased under Exs.P.47 to P.53 were
consolidated in Ex.P.46. To this effect, P.Dy. General Manager of U.T.I issued Ex.D.9—letter. In the cross-examination he admitted that
Exs.D.3 to D.8 are the accumulated dividend unit certificates. The contention of AO is that he only invested Rs.15,000/- and the said
investment fetched increased Unit Value which he reinvested. Therefore, the initial investment of Rs.15,000/- alone should be shown as
Asset. I.O admitted that he did not obtain any letter from UTI authorities that AO purchased units worth Rs.48,530.61. So in view of his
admission there is no material before the Court that AO originally purchased units worth Rs.48,530.61. What is shown under Exs.D.3
and D.4 is only a re-investment value but not initial purchase value. Hence, the Court below was right in taking Rs.15,000/- as the Asset
value. 10) Item No.16 - Movable articles as per Inventory – Rs.46,430/-: As per I.O, during the search of the residential premises of AO at
Visakhapatnam on 08.11.1987 in the presence of mediators, Ex.P.12—search list and Ex.P.13—Inventory report were prepared. At the
time of search, apart from the cash of Rs.54,600/-, Indira Vikas Patras, NSC certificates, gold articles and other household articles were
also found. They are noted in the Ex.P.13—Inventory report. According to IO, the value of the household articles as ascertained from AO
and his wife is Rs.46,430/-. The contention of AO is that the items were exorbitantly valued by the IO and some of the household articles
which were presented to the wife of AO by her parents as Sare and also some of the articles which belong to his two daughters kept in
his house were also added and at no point of time AO spent more than Rs.5000/- for his domestic items. The AO in order to establish his
stand examined DWs.1, 9 and 10 and filed Ex.D.17. DW.1—P.Vidyullatha is the second daughter of AO. She deposed about AO’s family
consisting of himself, his wife, four daughters and a son. She mainly deposed about the gold said to have been presented by the AO to
his daughters at the time of marriage and also their in-laws and their keeping gold with their parents. She did not speak about the
household articles. Only during re-examination she stated as if her elder sister and her husband while going to Kakinada and Gulbarga
respectively for doing P.G, sold heavy articles like cots and almarahs and kept the remaining movables and clothes at the house of her
parents. Then DW.9 who is the brother of AO’s wife deposed as if at the time of marriage of his sister in the year 1956, his parents
presented cash of Rs.20,000/-, silver articles and gold ornaments weighting 50 Tolas. So also the maternal grand father of AO presented
20 Tolas of gold jewellery to his sister. Further, the parents of AO presented 40 Tolas of gold jewellery to her. He further stated that when
AO setup his family in the year 1956 at the time of his first appointment, sare saamanu consisting of cots, beds, furnitures and several
household articles were given to his sister. Then DW.10, who is the Managing Partner of Sony Vision Electronics, Vijayawada deposed
that he gave Dynavox VCR to AO for his personal use when AO stated that his children came during 1987 and that he would return the
VCR after couple of weeks. He gave the VCR under letter Ex.D.17. About six months thereafter the AO returned the VCR. The trial Court
it appears believed the evidence of DW.1, DW.9 and DW.10 and deducted Rs.12,000/- towards the value of VCR, Rs.10,000/- as the
value of household articles of DW.1 and Rs.5,000/- towards the value of the Sare articles of wife of AO totalling Rs.27,000/-(Rs.10,000/-
+ Rs.12,000/- + Rs.5,000/-) and accepted the balance amount of Rs.19,430/- as the value of Item No.16 of the Assets. In the appeal,
Spl.P.P found fault with above deductions and contended that the trial Court allowed deductions on wrong appreciation of evidence. On
the other hand, learned Senior Counsel Sri T.Bali Reddy, supported the finding of the trial Court. In my considered view, the trial Court’s
concession in respect of two items i.e, value of VCR and value of Sare articles of AO’s wife cannot be accepted. VCR is concerned, in
Ex.P.72—explanation, AO did not mention that it belonged to DW.10 and that he only borrowed the VCR. Further, Ex.D.17—letter
purported to be given by DW.10 does not contain any date. Moreover, when AO borrowed the VCR, in normal course he should issue an
acknowledgement to DW.10 but not vice-versa as contained in Ex.D.17. So the VCR should be held to belong to AO and consequently
Rs.12,000/- cannot be deducted. Then Sare articles of the wife of AO are concerned, DW.9 has not specifically identified any of the
articles from Ex.P.13—list and stated that they were given to his sister. Moreover, according to him, Sare articles were presented in the
year 1956 and the search was conducted on 08.11.1987 i.e, more than 31 years after the presentation. It is hard to believe that all the
Sare articles would remain intact. Therefore, the said deduction of Rs.5,000/- is also untenable. The articles of DW.1 and her sister are
concerned, since her elder sister went to study P.G and DW.1 went to USA for job, it can be believed that they kept some of their
household articles with their parents. Further, during the cross-examination of I.O, it was specifically suggested that some of the
household articles belonged to his eldest daughter and son-in-law and they have stated this fact in their statements also and similarly
some of the articles belonged to his second daughter which she left with AO while leaving to USA. So the trial Court was right in
deducting Rs.10,000/- towards their articles. Thus the value of Item No.16 of the Assets comes to Rs.36,430/- (Rs.46,430/- minus
Rs.10,000/-). 11) Item No.17-Purchase of Gold Jewellery-Rs.1,18,066/-: As per PW.20—I.O, during house search of AO, the gold
jewellery and silverware were found and were got weighed through a goldsmith in the presence of AO and his wife and the weighment
and values were noted in the inventory as ascertained from the AO. The total value of gold jewellery purchased by AO worked out at
Rs.1,09,966/- and silverware at Rs.8,041/-, totalling Rs.1,18,007/-. The AO contended that his father-in-law presented 50 Tolas of gold
and 2 kgs of silver articles to his wife at the time of marriage and her maternal grand father gifted 20 tolas of gold while the parents of AO
presented 40 tolas of gold to the AO. It is further contended that gold and silver articles were purposefully valued excessively as if the AO
purchased the same during the check period. The AO to prove his case examined DWs.1, 4 and 9. DW.1 deposed that her elder sister
while going to Kakinada for doing P.G, retained only Thali Bottu, ear studs and bangles weighing 10 Tolas with her and kept the
remaining gold ornaments in her parents house for safety. So also DW.1 while going to USA to join her husband took with her gold
ornaments weighing 10 Tolas and kept remaining gold ornaments weighing 30 Tolas with her parents for safety. Then DW.4 and DW.9
are concerned, they deposed that at the time of marriage of AO, the parents of AO gave to his wife 40 Tolas of gold jewellery and so also
the maternal grand father of AO presented 20 Tolas of gold jewellery to his wife and the parents of AO’s wife gave Rs.20,000/- cash and
2 kgs of silver articles and 50 Tolas of gold. Then DW.7—an Accountant in Subramanyeshwari Jewellery Shop, Eluru was examined to
show that AO’s wife sold some silver under Ex.D.11—letter and gold under Exs.D.12 to D.14—receipts in their shop. Thus it was sought
to be projected by AO that AO’s wife dealt with the gold and silver presented to her by her parents and in fact AO did not purchase any
gold during the check period. In the cross-examination it was suggested by Spl.P.P that under Exs.D.12 to D.14, AO’s wife in fact
purchased the gold but not sold. It was elicited that the shop was maintaining account books for purchase and sale of gold and silver but
the said account book was not filed. The above is the evidence produced by the AO. The trial Court while appreciating the evidence of
DWs.4 and 9 observed that if really cash, gold and silver was given to AO’s wife at the time of marriage, the AO who was already in
service by that time should have declared the particulars of those valuables in his property statement submitted before his Higher
Authorities under Conduct Rules. But AO has not submitted his property statement. Further in Ex.P.72—explanation of AO, he stated
contrary to DWs.4 and 9 as if his father-in-law gave Rs.6,000/- and gold ornaments weighing about 40 tolas as Streedhana to his wife at
the time of marriage but in Ex.P.72 he has not stated anything about his maternal grandfather presenting gold ornaments weighing 20
tolas to his wife at the time of marriage. Thus the trial Court did not believe the presentation said to be made to the wife of AO at the time
of marriage. Then the evidence of DW.7 is concerned, the trial Court observed that the account books were not filed to show that the
shop owner purchased the gold and silver articles from the wife of AO. DW.1 is concerned, the trial Court has observed that the DW.1
has not stated the description of the gold ornaments and their respective weights, which her elder sister and herself kept in the house of
AO for safe custody. With the above observations generally the trial Court was expected to accept the entire value of Rs.1,18,007/- as the
asset of AO. However, the trial Court in Para 112 of its judgment appears to have given concession on the premise that some gold and
silver articles might have been given to AO’s wife at the time of his marriage, similarly, some gold and silver articles might have been
presented to the daughters of AO at their marriage which they might have kept with their parents and further, the I.O has not produced
any recorded evidence to show that AO purchased gold and silver articles and accordingly, accepted only Rs.30,000/- as the value of
the gold and silver articles of AO. Learned Spl.P.P severely criticised the above finding of the trial Court whereas learned Senior
Counsel supported the finding. In my considered view, the trial Court has given a vast concession erroneously which needs rectification.
Sofaras the gold and silver allegedly presented by AO’s parents, parents-in-law and grand father/ grand mother at the time of marriage is
concerned, no doubt DW.4—the brother of AO and DW.9—the brother-in-law of AO have given a uniform deposition. However, their
evidence cannot be accepted on the face value. It should be probed whether the AO made a declaration of the aforesaid alleged
presentations before concerned authorities to accept their evidence. Admittedly, AO did not file any record to show that he declared in
his property statement and I.T Returns about the alleged presentation of gold and silver by his relations. Even accepting the argument
that mere such non-declaration is not a ground to discard the presentations because the explanation of the phrase “known sources of
income” as contained in Sec.13(1)(e) is absent in Sec.5(1)(e) of Old Act, still they are liable to be discarded if he failed to disclose them

in his explanation under Exs.P.72 and P.85 before the ACB authorities. It must be noted that the appropriate time for AO to disclose
about the presentation of gold and silver is at the time of his offering explanation under Exs.P.72 and P.85 to the ACB authorities. So,
despite his non-disclosure in property statement and I.T Returns, if he made such disclosure in Exs.P.72 and P.85, some credence can
be given to his statement. In that context, when Exs.P.72 and P.85 are perused, in Ex.P.72 he mentioned to have received cash of
Rs.6,000/- and gold ornaments of 40 tolas from his parents-in-law and 30 Savarans of gold from his grand mother. Whereas in Ex.P.85
though he mentioned that his wife received gold ornaments, he did not give any particulars. Thus there is a vast variation between the
evidence of DWs.4 and 9 on one hand and Ex.P.72 on the other. Then sofaras gold ornaments allegedly kept by DW.1 and her sister
with AO are concerned, DW.1 could not state the description of the gold ornaments and their respective weights which her elder sister
and herself allegedly kept in the house of AO. Hence, the same is also liable to be included in the Asset of AO. It must be noted that in
the cross-examination of I.O, there was no specific suggestion that some of the gold and silver mentioned in Ex.P.13 belonged to the
daughters of AO and they left with him for safety. In that view of the matter, the evidence of DW.1 can only be taken as an afterthought.
The trial Court unfortunately has not taken above aspects into consideration and gave a vast concession as stated supra. Therefore,
giving some credence to Ex.P.72, the gold and jewellery of AO is valued at Rs.80,000/- and tacked to the Assets of AO. (II) INCOME: In
this appeal, both parties did not dispute Item Nos.1, 4 and 5. Hence, the remaining items are taken up for hearing. 12) ITEM No.1 - Net
Salary – Rs.3,69,675/-: As per charge-sheet and the evidence of PW.20, the net salary of AO during the check period was Rs.3,69,675/-.
The prosecution to establish this, relied upon the evidence of PW.10, PW.20 and Exs.P.25 to P.27, P.32 to P.37. Per contra, it appears,
the AO contended that his total salary during the relevant period was Rs.4,85,000/- and I.O deliberately did not show the actual income.
To buttress his contention AO examined DW.6 and produced Ex.D.20. Ex.P.25 is the statement showing pay particulars of AO from June
1995 to June 1996. Ex.P.26 is the salary particulars of AO from the month of November, 1987. Ex.P.27 is the salary particulars of AO
from December, 1976 to December, 1977. Ex.P.32 is the statement of pay particulars of AO from 01.01.1957 to 08.09.1957. Ex.P.33 is
the statement of pay particulars of AO from 17.09.1959 to 01.12.1962. Ex.P.34 is the statement of pay particulars of AO from 01.01.1963
to 13.02.1963. Ex.P.35 is the statement of pay particulars of AO from 18.02.1963 to 10.01.1964 Exs.P.36 and P.37 are the statements of
pay particulars of AO from 02.08.1984 to 01.08.1987. Basing on the above, the prosecution calculated AO’s salary at Rs.3,69,675/-. a)
As against the above, the AO examined DW.6—the Executive Engineer and through him produced Ex.D.20—the statement of pay
particulars from 01.01.1958 to 31.12.1988 for an amount of Rs.4,17,595.80/-. This statement was said to be issued by D.Rama Rao, the-then Executive Engineer, Miryalaguda. In the cross-examination DW.6 admitted that he do not know whether D. Rama Rao who signed
in Ex.D.20 was still in service or not and he also do not know who prepared Ex.D.20. He admitted that Ex.D.20 does not show the
deductions made from the salary of AO and other allowances. b) The judgments rendered by the trial judge and his successor judge after
remand would show that they have accepted the prosecution evidence and rejected the evidence of DW.6 and ultimately fixed the salary
income of AO as Rs.3,69,675/-. In this appeal, both prosecution and AO did not challenge the above finding. 13) ITEM No.2 –
Agricultural Income & sale of lands– Rs.2,54,796.45: The Agricultural Income and sale of agricultural lands put together was assessed
by the Investigating Officer at Rs.2,54,796.45. As per prosecution, the AO got Ac.5-07 cents land situated in Konatanapadu which he got
in partition in 1956 and the said land was with him upto 1987. Further, he got Ac.7-60 cents of land situated at Lankala Kalavagunta
village which was gifted to AO by his grandfather in the year 1950. From out of the above two types of lands he sold parcels of the land in
different years. The trial Court having taken these facts into consideration and also taking the respective yields and probable value of the
yields has arrived at the total value of the agricultural income including the sale consideration at Rs.5,00,000/-. In this appeal, now the
argument of learned Spl.P.P. is that the agricultural income arrived at by the trial Court is excessive as it took value of the agricultural
yield for different years on high side. He admitted that neither party produced the value of the crop from agricultural department. In this
context, a perusal of the judgment would show that the trial Court passed an elaborate order. While computing the agricultural income, it
fixed the average yield of paddy at 11 to 16 bags per acre per crop during the years 1956 to 1976 and 16 bags to 21 bags of paddy per
acre per crop during the years 1977 to 1987 for the lands situated in Kalavagunta and Konatanapadu villages. Having regard to the fact
that the lands are fed by Krishna canal water, the above estimate cannot be said to be on high side. Then coming to the prevailing rates
of the paddy during the relevant years, in Para 151 of its judgment the trial Court observed that neither party adduced any evidence in
that regard. Hence, the trial Court fixed the rate of paddy per bag (75 kgs) @ Rs.50/- for the years 1968 to 1970 and Rs.100/- per bag
from 1971 to 1987 on average. I find no exorbitance in the values adopted by the trial Court for computation of agricultural income.
Except claiming that the trial Court adopted high value, the prosecution has not produced the value of the yields issued by the
Agricultural Department even in this appeal to buttress its stand. Therefore, I find no force in this contention. 14) ITEM No.3 – Income
from house rents – Rs.90,710/-: As per I.O, house bearing No.26/378-A at Machilipatnam was constructed by AO during the year 1978-
1979 and it was let out for rent upto October 1987 @ Rs.310/- p.m. and So the said rent upto check period worked out to Rs.31,310/-.
Sofaras the flat bearing No.B-35/P/4 situated in Potti Sreeramulu Nagar, Vijay Nagar Colony, Hyderabad which stands in the name of
his daughter—G.Radhika is concerned, the claim of the AO that it fetched a total rent of Rs.59,400/- for check period was accepted. Thus
the IO arrived the total rental income at Rs.90,710/- (Rs.31,310/- + Rs.59,400/-) for the two houses. It appears, before the trial Court AO
contended that apart from the two houses, the mother of AO was having another house at Machilipatnam which was fetching rents and
the AO was having another ancestral house at Machilipatnam which was also fetching rents. Sofaras the ancestral house at
Machilipatnam and house of mother of AO are concerned, the trial Court has not taken into consideration since he has not mentioned the
same in Ex.P.72—explanation and also not suggested to I.O. Therefore, the trial Court taken into consideration only the house
constructed by AO at Machilipatnam and also the flat purchased by him in the name of his daughter—Radhika. Sofaras the house of AO
at Machilipatnam is concerned, the I.O calculated the rent @ Rs.310/- p.m but the trial Court accepted the same at Rs.450/- p.m and
arrived at the rental value at Rs.45,000/- from June, 1979 till 08.11.1987. The rental value of flat at Hyderabad is concerned, the value of
Rs.59,400/- as claimed by the AO was accepted. Thus the total rental value was arrived at Rs.1,04,400/- (Rs.45,000/- + Rs.59,400/-). In
this appeal, both parties have not challenged the above finding. (II-A) ADDITIONAL INCOME: 15) Item No.1 - Streedhana cash of the
wife of AO - Rs.20,000/-: We will find in the evidence of DW.4 and DW.9 to the effect that at the time of marriage of AO, his parents-in-law
presented Rs.20,000/- cash and 2 kgs of silver articles and gold ornaments weighing 50 tolas to his wife. Thus AO claimed the said
Rs.20,000/- as Streedhana property of his wife which she gave to him. The trial Court on the observation that in-laws of the AO are
financially sound and might presented some amount towards pasupukumkuma, accepted Rs.5,000/- as Streedhana property. In the
appeal, learned Spl.P.P castigated the above finding on the submission that the AO has not declared the above presentations in his
property statement submitted to the Higher Authorities and therefore, the same cannot be accepted. It is true that AO did not produce
record showing that he declared in his property statement about the above Streedhana. However, it is mentioned in Ex.P.72 about his
wife receiving Rs.6,000/- as Streedhana. Hence, in that view of the matter, the trial Court accepting Rs.5,000/- as Streedhana property
cannot be found fault. 16) Item No.3 – Agricultural Income of AO derived from the lands of his parents- Rs.10,00,000/-: According to AO,
his mother was owning 21 Acres of land as Streedhana property and his father was having Ac.3-41 cents and as they became old and
his younger brother deserted the family, the AO used to look after family agriculture from 1960 onwards and his parents used to give him
share and in that way during the check period he got agricultural income of about Rs.15 lakhs and therefore, a minimum of
Rs.10,00,000/- may be admitted as his agricultural income. To buttress his claim, the AO relied upon the evidence of DWs.4, 5 and 8 and
also Exs.D.1 and D.25. The contention of prosecution is that he did not divulge his alleged agricultural income from the lands of his
parents in Ex.P.72—explanation and hence his claim cannot be accepted. The trial Court basing on Exs.D.1 and D.25 held that the
parents of AO have lands. Then basing on the evidence of DW.8 held that he was assisting AO’s father in agricultural operations. Thus
the trial Court ultimately held that though in Ex.P.72 AO did not mention about his cultivating the lands of his parents, still the said fact
can be believed and accordingly allowed a sum of Rs.1,50,000/- as additional income of the AO towards agriculture. Impugning the
above finding, learned Spl.P.P argued in the appeal that the AO already claimed agricultural income under the heading Income and the
same was approved by the trial Court to an extent of Rs.5,00,000/- and in view of the same, his claim of further agricultural income is a
double claim and shall not be allowed. Per contra, learned Senior Counsel argued that the agricultural income from the lands owned by
the AO is quite different from the agricultural income derived by him by cultivating the lands of his parents and therefore, one cannot be
mistaken for the other and the trial Court rightly approved both the incomes separately. On a scrutiny of the evidence on record, I find
force in the submission of learned Senior Counsel. The agricultural income of Rs.5,00,000/- which was derived by the AO was from his

own lands which includes the income derived by sale of certain lands also. The present claim is in respect of the agricultural income
said to be derived by AO by cultivating the lands of his parents. Ex.D.25—certificate issued by MRO, Peddana Mandal would show the
mother of AO was owning Ac.19-12 cents of land. Similarly, Ex.D.1—proceedings of the Land Reforms Tribunal, Bandar would show
that AO’s father filed declaration in respect of his holding which was approved. Hence it is evident that the parents of AO were owning
agricultural lands. DW.8 deposed that after AO’s father became blind in 1966-67, AO started cultivating the lands of his mother. This
witness used to assist the father of AO, when he was cultivating the lands. So from all these, it can be believed that parents of AO had
lands and during their old age, AO looked after their cultivation and got some amount as cultivating share. Despite the said income is not
mentioned in the Ex.P.72, in view of overwhelming evidence, same can be accepted. The trial Court rightly held that AO was looking
after the agricultural operations of the lands of his parents and accordingly, admitted Rs.1,50,000/- as additional income of the AO. I find
no perversity in the appreciation of the evidence by the trial Court in this regard. (III) EXPENDITURE: In this appeal, the appellant
disputed items 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 10, 11 and 12. 17) Item No.1: Household expenditure-Rs.2,64,221/-: As per prosecution, the total family
expenditure of A.O during check period was Rs.2,64,221/-. To prove the said expenditure, the prosecution examined PW.19—G.
Laxminarayana, the-then Chartered Accountant for ACB. PW.19 deposed that he prepared Ex.P.61—expenditure statement of AO
basing on CSOs data and NINs coefficient, he arrived at a figure of Rs.2,57,297/- for the period covering 1960-1987 which includes
expenditure on food, clothing, shelter and education but excluding car maintenance and other luxuries. He mentioned in Ex.P.61 that IO
has to estimate expenditure for the year 1959 and earlier years. It appears, the IO got estimated the expenditure of AO for the years 1956
to 1959 in the same proportion and arrived at the total expenditure for the check period at Rs.2,64,221/-. a) Be that it may, the contention
of AO is that the expenditure in Ex.P.61 is highly excessive based on wrong assumptions without reference to the facts. It is contended
that in CSOs data there were as many as 33 items but PW.19 did not give any deductions in calculations for non-consuming and
unacceptable items. Citing the examples, it is contended that the days on which the accused was on camp was not excluded from the
calculation. Further, PW.19 has taken the wrong date of births of the children of AO which resulted in excess expenditure so also PW.19
has not made any allowance for deduction in his calculation for the years when the children of AO were away for studies staying in
hostels and so also no deduction was given even after three daughters of AO left the parental home after their marriages. Further, the AO
has agricultural background and led simple way of life being pure vegetarian and rice pulses and oils as well as vegetables were freely
available to them but no concession was given. Having regard to all these mistakes committed in calculation, the expenditure arrived at
by PW.19 is incorrect and only 40% of the salary of AO i.e, Rs.1,47,870/- has to be taken as his maximum domestic expenditure. b) Then
a perusal of judgments rendered by trial judge and his successor judge would show that basing on the evidence of PWs.19 and 20, they
have come to an opinion that certain shortcomings were there in the calculation of expenditure by PW.19. They noticed that PW.19 has
not taken into consideration the facts such as the three daughters during their study period have resided away from AO and further,
subsequent to their marriages they lived with their respective husbands during the check period. So also PW.19 did not take into
consideration that AO was residing in rent free quarters during the check period and did not incur expenditure. Further, PW.19 did not
give concession basing on the fact that AO was a vegetarian and hails from agricultural family and getting pulses rice and vegetables
etc., free of cost. Having come to such opinion, the trial judge before remand took 50% of net salary income of AO i.e, Rs.3,69,675/- as
his family expenditure. Whereas, his successor judge has taken 40% of the net salary of AO as his family expenditure. c) I have gone
through the evidence on record. It must be said that there are lapses on the part of PW.19 as pointed out by both the learned judges.
Therefore, AO deserves concession in calculating his family expenditure. Having regard to the fact that AO’s is a large family consisting
of himself, his wife, four daughters and a son and all the children were provided good education by sending to different places, in my
considered view, 50% of the net salary of AO can be deducted towards his family expenditure. Thus the family expenditure of AO comes
to Rs.1,84,837/- (Rs.3,69,675/- x 50%). 18) Item No.2 – Higher Education of AO’s Childrens – Rs.30,506/-: To prove the above
expenditure, the prosecution examined PWs.4, 9 and produced Exs.P.18, P.24, P.28 to P.31 and P.38 to P.40. PW.20—the I.O deposed
that the educational expenditure of the children of AO as per the above oral and documentary evidence comes to Rs.30,506/-. a) The
contention of AO is that it is a double entry since in Ex.P.61 the educational expenditure was already added by PW.19. b) The above
referred exhibits are the letters issued by the heads of the schools and colleges where the daughters of AO studied. There is force in the
contention of AO. As already stated earlier, in Ex.P.61, PW.19 mentioned that the expenditure figures include the expenditure on food,
clothes, shelter and education. Therefore, it shall be deemed that the educational expenditure now arrived at by the I.O was already
included in Item No.1—family expenditure. Further, the above exhibits would show that the study period of the children of AO was within
the check period. Therefore, their educational expenditure shall be deemed to be included in Item No.1. c) In the appeal, learned Spl.P.P
failed to explain how the above expenditure does not amount to duplication. Hence, this item of expenditure is deleted. 19) Item No.3 –
House Tax paid by AO for Machilipatnam House – Rs.7,251.40: The prosecution produced Exs.P.54 and P.55 to prove the said
expenditure. However, the Courts below deleted this item on the observation that taxes is one of the 33 items taken into consideration by
PW.19 for calculating the domestic expenditure under Ex.P.61. a) In this appeal, learned Spl.P.P though contended that taxes should be
treated as separate expenditure but failed to explain that the said expenditure was not already included in Ex.P.61. Hence, this item of
expenditure is deleted. 20) Item No.4 – Treatment of AO and his wife – Rs.20,000/-: As per I.O, AO was suffering with heart disease and
his wife was suffering with Asthma and during 1983 he was admitted in Sindura Nursing Home, Nellore and he took treatment from Dr.
Ramchandra Ready for about 1½ months and his wife undergone treatment for Asthma under Dr.Satyanarayana of Eluru. Accordingly,
I.O assessed the medical expenditure at Rs.20,000/-. The contention of AO is that the medical expenditure was already included in Item
No.1—family expenditure under Ex.P.61. The trial Court deleted the above expenditure on two grounds. Firstly that PW.20 has not
gathered any evidence such as medical bills to prove the medical expenditure of AO and his wife. Secondly, on the ground that “medical
care” was one of the 33 items of expenditure included in Ex.P.61. a) In the appeal, learned Spl.P.P argued that though I.O did not collect
evidence such as medical bills etc., still in Ex.P.72—explanation AO admitted about the treatment of his wife and himself and therefore, a
reasonable medical expenditure can be taken into consideration. He argued that under Ex.P.61, the basic items are food, clothing,
shelter and education only and medical care is not shown in it. b) On perusal of the evidence, I am unable to accept the contention of
learned Spl.P.P. No doubt in Ex.P.72, AO mentioned that he suffered heart attack in April, 1983 and admitted in Sindura Nursing Home
in Nellore and took treatment from Dr. Ramchandra Reddy for about 1 ½ months and later in Apollo Hospital, Madras. He also
mentioned that his wife is an Asthma patient and undergoing treatment from Dr.Satyanarayana, Eluru. However, he did not mention the
medical expenditure. The I.O has not gathered any evidence in this regard. So there is no basis for the figure of Rs.20,000/- shown by
I.O. Even assuming that AO incurred the said expenditure as claimed by I.O, the same must be deemed to be included under Ex.P.61,
because medical expenditure is one of the inevitable expenditure incurred by every individual. Hence, the said item is deleted. 21) Item
No.6 – Income Tax paid by AO – Rs.4,903/-: The prosecution to prove this expenditure produced Exs.P.80 and P.81—made up files
containing Income Tax papers of the AO. The AO contended that this item of expenditure was already included in Ex.P.61 and having
accepted his contention, the trial Court deleted this item of expenditure. a) In this appeal, learned Spl.P.P. though sought to treat this
expenditure separately, could not be able to convince this Court as to how such treatment will not amount to double expenditure. Hence,
his request is rejected. 22) Item No.7 – Maintenance of House – Rs.15,110/-: As per I.O, the AO constructed house bearing No.26/378/A
in A.P Housing Colony, Machilipatnam and received total rent of Rs.31,310/- during the check period and so also he got allotted plot
bearing No.B-35/F4 in Vizianagaram Colony, Hyderabad and for maintenance of these two houses, he incurred an expenditure of
Rs.15,118.33 which is roughly 1/6th of the total rent. The trial Court deleted this item of expenditure on the premise that the said
expenditure was already included in one of the 33 items under Ex.P.61. I see no reason to differ with the said finding. 23) Item No.10 –
Marriage expenditure of three daughters of AO– Rs.1,06,000/-: Basing on the statement of AO under Ex.P.72, the I.O estimated the
marriage expenditure of the three daughters of AO at Rs.1,06,000/-. The contention of AO is that all his three daughters are well-educated, qualified and good-looking and hence the bridegrooms and their parents approached on their own accord and settled the
marriages without any formalities and hence the expenditure incurred for the marriage of each daughter did not exceed Rs.10,000/- and
so he had no objection for taking the marriage expenditure as Rs.30,000/- in all. The record shows that in the judgment prior to remand,
the trial judge having considered the facts mentioned in Ex.P.72 to the effect that the first daughter—Usha Rani’s marriage was
performed in August, 1980 with one Dr. Venkata Ramaiah in Padmavathi Kalyana Mandapam, Vijayawada and AO presented 5
Savarans of gold ornaments to her at the time of marriage and further, AO performed marriage of his second daughter—G. Vidyullatha in

April, 1984 in Natraj Hotel, Vijayawada with one Siva Shankar and he presented 5 Savarans of gold ornaments to her at the time of
marriage and he performed the marriage of his third daughter—Radhika with Satya Murthy, IAS in September, 1985 also in Natraj Hotel,
Vijayawada and he presented 5 Savarans of gold ornaments to her at the time of marriage, opined that the figure of Rs.1,06,000/- arrived
at by the PW.20—I.O as the marriage expenditure of the three daughters cannot be said to be on high side and accordingly accepted the
same. However, learned Successor Judge fixed the marriage expenditure of three daughters of AO at Rs.60,000/- on the observation
that neither prosecution nor the AO produced any cogent evidence to buttress their respective claims. a) In the appeal, learned Spl.P.P
argued that having regard to the fact that the marriages of all the three daughters were performed between 1980 and 1985 with
bridegrooms of high social status at Vijayawada, one in Kalyana Mandapam and two in Natraj Hotel and each daughter was presented
with 5 Savarans of gold at the time of marriage, a total marriage expenditure of Rs.1,06,000/- by no means can be said to be on high
side. b) I find force in the aforesaid argument. A holistic calculation of the marriage expenditure of the three daughters will certainly
present a figure of Rs.1,06,000/- i.e, on an average of Rs.35,000/- for each marriage. In view of the fact that the daughters of AO were
married to grooms of high social status and generally large gathering will be there at each marriage and further, the three marriages were
performed in between 1980 and 1985 by which time the prices of all commodities started skyrocketing and also that the marriages were
not performed at brides’ home but in Kalyana Mandapam and Hotel at Vijayawada and further, each of the three daughters was
presented with 5 Savarans of gold by the AO, the total marriage expenditure of Rs.1,06,000/- as arrived by I.O cannot be said to be
excessive. Hence, the marriage expenditure of the three daughter is accepted as Rs.1,06,000/-. 24) Item No.11 – Payment of land
revenue taxes – Rs.10,652.72/-: In Ex.P.85—explanation given by AO, he admitted to have incurred land revenue tax of Rs.10,652/-.
However, considering the fact that taxes is one of the 33 items included in Ex.P.61, both the trial judge and his Successor Judge have
deleted this expenditure. I find no reason to differ with them. 25) Item No.12 – Maintenance of Dog–Rs.17,550/-: As per prosecution, the
AO spent about Rs.17,550/- for maintenance of his dog. However, basing on Ex.P.85-explanation of AO, he contended that his children
collected a cross-breed dog in May, 1985 and they gave the left over vegetarian food in the house and one egg per day and thus the
ration given to the dog per day would not be more than Rs.1.50ps. and thus the cost of maintenance of the dog from 1985-87 was only
Rs.1,095/-. However, the prosecution based its claim on the evidence of PW.12—Asst. Director of Veterinary Hospital and his report
Ex.P.45. PW.12 on examination of the dog estimated its expenditure at Rs.585/- p.m. In the cross-examination he admitted that his
calculation was based on the ration required for the dog and he admitted that the persons running kennels and shepherds are better
persons to speak about the expenditure of the dog and if the ration mentioned in Ex.P.45 was not given, the maintenance cost would be
much-less. a) Basing on the above evidence on record, both the learned Judges have fixed the maintenance expenditure of dog at
Rs.3,600/- i.e, @ Rs.100/- p.m for three years. This fixation being reasonable, I approve the same. 26) Pursuant to the above discussion,
the abstract of assets, income and expenditure of AO is as follows: TABLE-I S. No.Particulars of assetsValue as per Prosecution Rs.
Ps.Value as per A.O Rs. Ps.Value arrived by trial Judge after remand Rs. Ps.Value arrived in this appeal Rs. Ps.1234561Construction of
double storied house at Plot No.12 in NTR Colony, Gunadala4,00,500-002,77,000-003,50,000-003,50,500-002Construction of house
No.26/378/A in A.P Housing Colony, Machilipatnam93,000-0024,000-0093,000-0093,000-003Purchased Flat No.B-35/F4 in P.S Nagar,

15,110-33------8Stamp duty and registration charges13,209-87--13,209-8713,209-879Maintenance of Car AJH 979200-00200-00200-
00200-0010Marriage expenditure of three daughters1,06,000-0030,000-0060,000-001,06,000-0011Payment of land revenue
taxes10,652-72------12Maintenance of dog17,550-00--3,600-003,600-00Total5,23,039-822,13,635-502,58,315-373,41,282-87 27) Now,
following the unreported judgment dated 04.12.2013 of this High Court in the case of P.Dayaratnam vs. State, ACB, Nellore cited by
respondent/ AO, 10% addition is made as a cushion to the income of the AO. In which event, the ultimate financial statement of the AO
works out as follows: FINANCIAL STATEMENT OF AO S.NoParticularsThis Court finding Rs. Ps.1.a) Assets:10,77,161-00b)
Income:11,69,488-00 1,16,948-80 (10% added as per case law) 12,86,436-80c) Expenditure:3,41,282-87Result: a-(b-c)Disproportionate
Assets of 1,32,007-07 28) Thus the disproportionate assets of AO come to Rs.1,32,007.07. It may be noted that the respondent/AO relied
upon the P.Dayaratnam’s case (2 supra) also on the point that in case the disproportionate assets are less than 10% of total income of
the AO, it would not be justifiable to raise presumption under Sec.5(3) of the P.C. Act, 1947 and punish him. In that case, this High Court
followed the judgment of Apex Court reported in B.C.Chaturvedi vs. Union of India and others (vide Paragraph 16). It should be
mentioned here that even if the aforesaid decision is applied to the present case, there will be no advantage to respondent/AO since his
disproportionate assets are more than 10% of his total income. 29) So on a conspectus of the entire facts and evidence on record, the AO
is found guilty of the charge under Sec.5(1)(e) r/w 5(2) of P.C. Act, 1947 and he is convicted accordingly and consequently, the judgment
of the trial Court in C.C.No.43 of 1992 is set aside by allowing this Criminal Appeal. ___________________________ U. DURGA
PRASAD RAO, J Date: 08.06.2015 scs 30) On the quantum of sentence, following the decision of Hon’ble Apex Court reported in
Suryamoorthi and another vs. Govindaswamy and others, this Court prefers to hear the respondent/AO before passing sentence. Today,
the respondent/AO is not present in Court. Sri K. Suresh Reddy, learned Counsel for respondent/AO, submits that AO is not the local
resident and he will inform to him to be present in Court and sought time. Hence, the matter is posted to 17.06.2015 for hearing the
respondent/AO on quantum of sentence to be imposed. ___________________________ U. DURGA PRASAD RAO, J Date:
08.06.2015 scs (Contd..…) Quantum of Sentence in Criminal Appeal No. 1387 of 2007: Today, the Respondent/AO by name Sri
G.V.Gopala Rao is present. Learned Senior Counsel—Sri T. Bali Reddy representing Sri K.Suresh Reddy, learned counsel for the
respondent/AO is present. Heard the respondent/AO on the quantum of sentence to be imposed. He submitted that he is now 82 years
old and suffering with old age ailments like Diabetes and also debility and that earlier he underwent bypass surgery and that he is alone
residing at Machilipatnam and his wife died and considering these circumstances, lenience may be shown in imposing the sentence. It is
seen that the offence of Criminal Misconduct under Section 5(1)(e) r/w 5 (2) of P.C.Act, 1947 is punishable with a minimum sentence of
One year and extendable to Seven years and also liable to fine. However, the proviso to sub-Section (2) says that the Court may for
special reasons recorded in writing impose a sentence of imprisonment of less than one year. Now, having regard to the nature of the

offence and the circumstances under which AO committed and having regard to quantum of disproportionate assets amassed by him
and also considering his submission in Court today that he is an old man and suffering with old age ailments and he already lost his
wife, this Court is of the view that imposition of minimum sentence of less than One year will meet the ends of justice. Accordingly, the
respondent/AO is sentenced to undergo Simple Imprisonment for a period of Six(6) months and also to pay a fine of Rs.60,000/- (Rupees
Sixty Thousand only) and in default of payment of fine, shall undergo Simple Imprisonment for a period of two(2) months. The remand
period, if any, shall be given set off. The Respondent/AO is informed of his right to appeal. The Registry is directed to provide a free copy
of this Judgment to the Respondent/AO. The Respondent/AO is directed to surrender before the Trial Court on or before 02-07-2015 and
on such surrender, the Trial Court shall commit him to jail for serving sentence. _________________________ U. DURGA PRASAD
RAO, J Dt: 17.06.2015 Note: Office is directed to dispatch this judgment forthwith. (b/o) eha 2002 Crl.L.J.2982 = Manu/SC/1224/2001
